===== EPISODE 1: The Green Circle Transfer Student =====

# Episode 1: **The Green Circle Transfer Student**

The morning sky over **Aurelis City** caught the sun in layered gold, each cloud edge refracting mana into angles light should not have known.

Pale gold light stretched across the clouds, and between the white towers and glass bridges of the capital, floating transport sigils shimmered in neat lines through the air. Suspended carriages passed soundlessly above the avenues. Fountains in the lower plazas rose in spirals shaped by spellcraft rather than pressure. Even the lamps along the streets glowed with a soft, living pulse, responding to the movement of people beneath them.

In this world, magic was not rare.

It was architecture.

It was law.

It was reputation.

And more than anything, it was hierarchy.

Every person awakened a **Magic Circle** in childhood. In lower schools, children learned letters, arithmetic, and the first rules of mana control beside a quieter lesson no one wrote on the board: the color near your heart changed how loudly adults listened when you spoke.

When mana matured, a crest appeared close to the heart, revealing the rank that would define how the world treated them.

**Green Circle** magicians were the majority, nearly **60% of the population**, and were considered the foundation of society-though in truth, most people used kinder words to hide how little they respected them. Most graduated into maintenance guilds, ward upkeep, transport routing, household enchantment work, or support offices where competence was expected and credit traveled upward.  
**Blue Circle** magicians, about **30%**, were valued for their competence and often filled skilled roles in magical industries, city management, and technical professions.  
**Red Circle** magicians, only **9%**, were the elite-leaders, heirs, scholars, executives, strategists, and the people whose names carried weight before they entered a room.  
And **Yellow Circle** magicians, the rare **1%**, stood at the top of known human power, admired with reverence and feared with equal sincerity.

The official story came polished by centuries of academy charters and civic primers until hierarchy sounded almost natural.

The world liked neat categories.

The truth was less convenient.

A stronger Circle did not always guarantee victory. Skill mattered. Discipline mattered. Training, control, intelligence, instinct, and the ability to keep a clear mind under pressure mattered. A Green Circle magician with enough experience could defeat a Blue. A Blue could overcome a Red under the right conditions. Even a Yellow could lose if arrogance made them careless.

But society preferred to pretend rank was destiny.

It made discrimination easier.

And hidden beneath that entire system, buried in myth and ancient fragments of forbidden theory, there existed one more Circle that no one recognized as real.

**Black Circle.**

A level said to be beyond Yellow so completely that comparison became meaningless.

A celestial rank.

A power no living scholar had ever documented.

A theory.

A rumor.

A lie, according to almost everyone alive.

Except it wasn't a lie.

Because I existed.

My name was **Haru**.

And on the morning I arrived at **Aurelis Arcane Academy**, one of the most prestigious elite high schools in the world, I was carrying enough power to bend reality itself while pretending to be the weakest kind of magician society could tolerate.

A Green Circle.

I stood before the academy gates and adjusted the collar of my new uniform.

The silver-blue crest of Aurelis was stitched neatly into the breast pocket. The fabric was expensive, durable, and enchanted against minor elemental damage. It was the daily uniform, cut for movement and classroom work rather than ceremony, but even that carried more polish than most district schools reserved for graduation.

The kind of uniform meant to remind students that this institution did not merely teach greatness-it expected it.

The gate's old ward pressure settled behind my sternum, cold enough to make my breathing careful.

Ahead of me, the academy rose like a monument to status.

Tall crystal windows reflected the sunlight in fractured colors. The main building stood beneath a domed roof encircled by six rune-marked towers. White stone stairways led through a courtyard lined with silver-leaf trees and statues of famous graduates. Floating glyphs rotated above the entrance, displaying academic honors, tournament victories, and lists of alumni who now governed cities, corporations, and magical institutions.

It was beautiful in the way only old power could be.

And it was exactly the kind of place where a secret like mine could become a disaster.

A quiet pulse stirred against my chest.

I pressed two fingers lightly over my heart.

Beneath my shirt, hidden by layers of advanced mana concealment, my true Magic Circle slept in silence.

It was not bright.

It did not blaze like Yellow or radiate with noble warmth like Red.

It was darker than shadow, precise as law, and vast enough that even fully suppressed it made the air around me feel subtly wrong if I let my concentration slip. The circle was layered with impossible geometry-patterns too old, too complete, too absolute for modern magical theory to interpret.

Black Circle.

Celestial level.

Enough power to erase armies, stop storms, or fracture the magical order of the world itself.

I had no intention of doing any of those things.

I had spent years learning how to be small.

How to suppress.

How to hide.

How to live in a world that would either worship, weaponize, or fear me if it learned what I really was.

Mana flowed from my fingertips in a whisper.

The illusion settled.

Anyone who examined me now would see a modest **Green Circle crest**. Slightly weak. Slightly unimpressive. Exactly the kind of rank people overlooked unless they were looking for someone to demean.

Perfect.

I took a breath and stepped through the gates.

The stares started almost immediately.

Not everyone noticed me. Some students were busy greeting one another. Some were checking class schedules in the floating directory panels. Some were walking in clusters by color and social position, their conversations polished and easy.

No one walked beside me.

But enough eyes shifted toward me that the pattern became obvious within seconds.

They knew.

Transfer student.  
Mid-year entry.  
Green Circle.

At a school like Aurelis, those three facts could become a topic before I reached my first classroom.

I kept walking.

Near the fountain to my right, a pair of Blue Circle boys lowered their voices just enough to show they wanted to be overheard.

Blue Student: "Green?"

Blue Student: "At Aurelis?"

Blue Student: "Maybe admissions made a mistake."

Ahead of me, two Red Circle students stepped aside to allow a Yellow Circle girl and her attendants to pass. One of the Reds noticed me, paused, and let out a short laugh.

Red Student: "That's new."

Red Student: "Try not to get lost. This school can be dangerous for ordinary people."

A few nearby students smiled.

I walked past them without reacting.

Even so, it bothered me.

Not for my own sake. I had heard worse, in colder places, from people with more authority and less restraint. But casual contempt had a way of sinking into the walls of institutions. It taught people where they were allowed to stand. It taught them what treatment they were expected to accept.

Cruelty became culture that way.

The entrance hall opened around me like a cathedral built for magical aristocracy.

A domed ceiling glimmered with artificial constellations. Black stone floors reflected the light of floating chandeliers. Twin staircases curved upward toward the upper academic wings, and golden mana lines ran beneath the floor in elegant channels, feeding power to the building's barrier systems and internal enchantments.

A luminous panel appeared in front of me.

**WELCOME, TRANSFER STUDENT: HARU AERLEN**  
**YEAR TWO - SECTION THREE**  
**HOMEROOM: EAST WING, THIRD FLOOR**

Instructor Selene: "Haru Aerlen?"

I turned.

A woman in a formal navy academy coat approached with a rune-glass tablet in hand. Her dark hair was tied back neatly, and a slender **Red Circle** crest glowed near the base of her throat. Her expression was composed, intelligent, and slightly tired in the way of people who managed too many strong personalities every day.

Instructor Selene: "I'm Instructor Selene Varis. Student Affairs. You're the transfer from Lumeris District?"

Haru: "Yes."

Instructor Selene: "Come with me. Homeroom has already started."

I nodded and followed her into the east corridor.

The deeper we went, the more obvious the academy's invisible ranking system became. Rooms reserved for top classes were closer to better mana conduits. Certain hallways displayed only Red and Yellow alumni portraits. The walls themselves carried subtle status markers-honors, access permissions, and internal privileges built around the assumption that stronger circles deserved better spaces.

In a study alcove near the eastern stair, a Green Circle student raised one hand to correct a scheduling mistake and was cut off mid-sentence by a Blue who did not even look at him. The Green apologized automatically, as if being interrupted had been his error.

Selene noticed where I was looking.

Instructor Selene: "Aurelis rewards excellence."

I wondered how many people heard that sentence and never asked who got to define excellence in the first place.

Haru: "I see."

She glanced at me.

Instructor Selene: "This school is not easy, Haru. Students here are ambitious. Competitive. Most come from influential families. You may face resistance."

Haru: "That's expected."

A flicker of approval passed through her expression.

Instructor Selene: "Your admission was unusual. Your Circle ranking caused concern. But your written examination scores were extraordinary. Theory, tactical ethics, mana structures, ancient script analysis-someone argued strongly in your favor."

Haru: "Someone?"

Instructor Selene: "I was not told who."

Mildly troubling.

I had intentionally tried not to stand out.

Apparently I had failed.

She stopped before a pair of double doors.

Inside, a lecture was already underway.

Selene opened the doors and stepped aside.

The classroom beyond was spacious and bright, with ascending rows of curved desks and large windows along one wall. At the front, a broad-shouldered instructor stood beside a floating diagram of nested spell circles.

One student glanced up.

Then another.

Conversation thinned in uneven layers as my crest became visible.

Instructor Dren: "The transfer?"

Instructor Selene: "Yes. Haru Aerlen."

Instructor Dren: "Introduce yourself."

Thirty students watched me.

I felt their mana signatures instinctively. Several Blues. Two Reds. A few Greens holding themselves small. No Yellow Circle in this class, though I suspected there would be some elsewhere in the year.

Aurelis sorted homerooms by academic track instead of rank, officially. The seating chart suggested rank still found its way into the room.

Haru: "I'm Haru Aerlen. I transferred in today. I'll do my best."

Desks creaked as students leaned toward one another.

Student: "The mid-year transfer is Green?"

Laughter spread in pockets around the room.

Red Student: "Interesting standards this year."

Student: "Did they expand support placement or lose a scholarship file?"

Instructor Dren knocked his knuckles once against the board.

Instructor Dren: "That's enough."

The class settled, though not with much grace.

Instructor Dren: "Back row. Open seat beside Aira."

As I moved through the rows, I caught a folded paper pellet before it hit my shoulder. I let it drop anyway. No reason to make that look deliberate.

At the back sat a girl with warm brown eyes and chestnut hair tied loosely to one side. Her **Blue Circle** crest glowed on the inside of her wrist as she shifted her notebook. She looked at me with curiosity, not superiority.

That alone made her stand out.

Aira: "Hi. I'm Aira Solen."

Haru: "Haru."

Aira: "Nice to meet you."

There was no mockery in it.

No hidden edge.

Just kindness.

That felt unexpectedly rare.

At the front, Instructor Dren resumed the lecture.

Instructor Dren: "As I was saying, magical society classifies human mana potential into four recognized Circle ranks."

Four luminous crests appeared in the air.

**Green. Blue. Red. Yellow.**

He began to walk beneath them as he spoke.

Instructor Dren: "Green Circle magicians make up the majority of the population. They possess the lowest average mana reserves and the narrowest output margin. However, low raw capacity does not make them useless. Much of society would not function without their labor, service, technical support, and stable contributions to ordinary magical systems."

A few students listened. A few smirked.

Instructor Dren: "Blue Circle magicians demonstrate stronger reserves, broader practical application, and greater combat or industrial flexibility. They comprise much of the skilled professional class."

Leon Varek, a tall Blue Circle boy near the center, sat a little straighter as if the category itself had just complimented him personally. His blond hair was swept back with deliberate care, his uniform cuffs were fastened with small silver clasps, and his water-affinity crest glowed pale blue against the inside of his wrist whenever he moved.

Instructor Dren: "Red Circle magicians possess high reserves, refined affinity potential, and superior adaptability. They disproportionately occupy positions of leadership in academia, politics, research, and business."

One of the Red Circle students near the front crossed her legs and looked faintly pleased.

Instructor Dren: "And Yellow Circle magicians represent the highest recognized level of mana development known to modern society. Their numbers are extremely low, but their influence is considerable. Most institutions go years without seeing more than one."

The four circles rotated slowly above him.

Green. Blue. Red. Yellow.

The four colors washed over thirty faces.

I raised my hand.

That alone seemed to surprise people.

Instructor Dren: "Yes, transfer student?"

I kept my expression neutral.

Haru: "What about a Black Circle magician?"

Aira turned toward me so quickly her pen scratched across the margin of her notes.

Two students blinked. Someone near the front mouthed the words back as if testing whether they became less absurd silently.

Then the laughter started.

Not everyone laughed, but enough did that the sound filled the classroom in a wave.

Student: "Black Circle?"

Student: "What is this, a storybook?"

Student: "I knew Greens had weird theories, but that's embarrassing."

Leon: "Are we discussing children's myths now?"

Red Student: "You might as well ask about star dragons and moon gods."

Aira did not laugh.

She just watched me, curious.

Instructor Dren held up one hand, and the noise dropped away.

His face did not show mockery, only the patience of a teacher deciding how seriously to answer a strange question.

Instructor Dren: "In formal magical theory, there is no recognized Black Circle classification."

He turned slightly and with a motion of his hand summoned a second image beside the four crests-fragments of ancient script, incomplete diagrams, broken rings.

Instructor Dren: "However, in very old texts there are occasional references to anomalous or transcendent mana structures beyond the standard system. Most scholars consider these symbolic, religious, or metaphorical. Some regard them as errors in preservation. A minority of fringe theorists have speculated that an unknown superior rank may once have been hypothesized."

The fragments dissolved and reformed.

Instructor Dren: "But there is no verified historical evidence that any human has ever possessed such a circle. In academic practice, Black Circle is not treated as reality."

Haru: "So it's impossible?"

Dren folded his arms.

Instructor Dren: "In magic, 'impossible' is often a temporary word. But until something can be observed, measured, or reproduced, it remains speculation. Your task as students is to understand what exists, not chase every legend that flatters the imagination."

His gaze passed over the room before settling briefly on Leon's row.

Instructor Dren: "And understanding what exists includes understanding limits. Power without discipline is not mastery. It is noise with a crest attached."

The class relaxed again.

Someone chuckled.

Leon: "There you have it."

Student: "Imagine transferring in and asking about myths."

Aira glanced between me and the front of the room.

Aira: "Bold first-day question."

Haru: "I was curious."

Her eyes lingered on me for a second longer than necessary.

Aira: "Somehow, I don't think curiosity carried all of that."

I did not answer.

At the front, Dren dismissed the projection and continued.

Instructor Dren: "Remember this. Circle rank reflects potential, not guaranteed victory. A stronger Circle can still lose to a weaker opponent with superior control, training, timing, or tactical judgment. Raw mana matters. It is not everything."

That, at least, was true.

Beside me, Aira resumed her notes.

I left my notebook mostly blank and listened.

The rest of the lecture covered layered output, mana stability under emotional strain, and the structural limits of reinforcement casting across different circle ranks. It was competent instruction, though bounded by assumptions so deeply embedded in modern magical education that no one in the room seemed to notice them.

I noticed all of them.

Not because I was smarter than everyone there.

Just because I lived outside the system they thought explained the whole world.

When the bell rang, conversation returned immediately.

Aira turned to me first.

Aira: "If you need help finding the practical arena, I can show you."

Before I could answer, a shadow fell across our desks.

Leon Varek stood there with a smile polished enough to be mistaken for friendliness by people who wanted the world simple.

Leon: "Aira, we're still meeting at the west courtyard before practical assessment."

Aira: "I remember."

His eyes moved to me.

Leon: "And you must be in our practical block."

Haru: "I am."

He nodded as if processing unfortunate news.

Leon: "Well, try not to do anything reckless. Some exercises aren't designed for low-capacity circles. I'd hate for your first day to become a cautionary tale."

Haru: "I'll be careful."

He looked almost disappointed that I had not taken the bait.

Aira: "Leon."

Leon: "What? New students should know the risks."

Then he straightened and left.

Aira: "He does that when he wants an audience."

Haru: "He seems efficient about it."

That made her laugh.

A small laugh. Real.

For some reason, that stayed with me longer than Leon's smug warning.

The bell for transition echoed through the corridor a few minutes later, and the class began moving toward the eastern training complex in clusters. As expected, most students naturally grouped by familiarity and rank. Red Circles walked with the casual confidence of those accustomed to being admired. Blue Circles filled the spaces around them, loud enough to be heard, eager enough to be noticed. Green Circles moved more carefully, often in pairs or alone, keeping to the edges of the hallway.

A second-year in a green-trimmed uniform stepped aside to let a Red Circle group pass and nearly collided with an automated cleaning glyph. He apologized to the glyph before he apologized to himself.

I walked beside Aira.

Not close enough for it to look intentional. Close enough that people would still notice.

That alone seemed to annoy Leon, who kept glancing back from the group ahead.

The training complex was larger than the classroom blocks, built in white stone reinforced with metallic mana-thread and engraved from foundation to ceiling with stabilization runes. The main assessment hall opened into a circular arena surrounded by tiered observation platforms. Transparent barriers shimmered faintly around the combat floor, powered by crystal pylons set at equal intervals along the perimeter.

It was the kind of place built for talent to be displayed.

And for weakness to be exposed.

Students filtered into the stands while Instructor Dren stepped into the center of the arena, his boots clicking once against the polished stone. With a flick of his wrist, a floating panel appeared above him displaying the day's objective.

**PRACTICAL ASSESSMENT: MANA RESPONSE AND COMBAT ADAPTABILITY**

Dren folded his arms.

Instructor Dren: "Listen carefully. This is not a ranking match and not an excuse to indulge personal grudges. Today's objective is to evaluate control under pressure, reaction speed, adaptive judgment, and efficient use of output."

He gestured, and several lines of text rearranged themselves beneath the title.

Instructor Dren: "You will engage in supervised one-on-one assessments. Victory is not determined by brute force alone. Points are awarded for tactical decisions, precision, composure, restraint, and proper use of the arena rules."

Red-Haired Student: "So overwhelming your opponent immediately is discouraged."

Instructor Dren: "It is, when your idea of overwhelming them includes reconstructive healing."

A few students laughed.

Instructor Dren: "Direct lethal intent will result in immediate disqualification and disciplinary review. Excessive force lowers your score. Loss of emotional control lowers your score. If you have enough skill to win elegantly and choose chaos instead, I will assume your education has failed somewhere."

The last line did more work than the official rules.

I felt a faint shift in the air beside me.

Aira: "He says things like that a lot."

Haru: "Does it help?"

Aira: "Not as much as he hopes."

I smiled faintly.

Almost.

Dren raised one hand, and a new panel of names appeared in the air.

Pairs began forming.

One by one, students reacted with varying degrees of satisfaction, annoyance, or concealed panic. Friends avoided each other's eyes. Rivals sat up straighter. A few Greens visibly tensed when assigned to stronger Blues.

Then my name appeared.

**HARU AERLEN vs LEON VAREK**

The room shifted immediately.

A ripple of interest spread through the arena. Some students laughed without bothering to hide it. Others exchanged knowing looks, already deciding how quickly the match would end.

Leon himself placed a hand over his chest in mock disbelief.

Leon: "Sir, I'm not sure this is fair."

Instructor Dren: "For whom?"

That drew louder laughter.

Leon: "I only mean a Blue Circle against a first-day Green transfer may create a certain impression."

Instructor Dren: "It will. Sit in the arena, not in your ego."

Aira turned to me at once.

Her expression had changed. Not pity. Concern.

Aira: "You don't have to prove anything."

Haru: "Yes."

Aira: "Leon likes to humiliate people when he thinks he has an audience."

Haru: "I noticed."

Aira: "He's strong, but he performs. If you stay calm and make him choose his angles, you might last longer than he expects."

Manage longer.

A fair thing for her to say, given what she believed I was.

Haru: "Thank you."

Her surprise was small, but I caught it: a quick lift of the brows, then a careful nod, as if kindness had not been the answer she expected from me either.

Leon dropped from the platform into the arena with easy confidence. Blue mana curled around his hands like ribbons of water catching sunlight. He played to the audience naturally-not in big theatrical gestures, but in subtle ways. The relaxed smile. The way he rolled his shoulders. The measured confidence that suggested this would be quick.

I stepped down into the arena opposite him.

The barrier sealed above us with a low hum.

Immediately, I became aware of every eye in the room.

The Greens watched carefully.
The Blues watched eagerly.
The Reds watched with detached curiosity.
Aira watched with narrowed focus.

Instructor Dren stood just outside the barrier and raised one hand.

Instructor Dren: "Assessment begins on my signal. First clean disruption of three balance markers, forced surrender, or incapacitation ends the match. Remember the rules."

Leon smiled at me.

Leon: "You can yield before we start. No one would blame you."

Haru: "I'm all right."

His smile sharpened.

Dren dropped his hand.

Instructor Dren: "Begin."

Leon moved first.

A stream of compressed water mana shot toward me in a clean horizontal line, fast enough to force a reaction but controlled enough to avoid serious injury. It was a testing strike-something to establish distance and make me move badly.

I stepped aside.

The stream cut past me and crashed harmlessly against the far barrier, exploding into mist.

Leon did not pause. A second stream followed immediately, then a third from a different angle, curving inward to limit my options. Good sequencing. Better than average for someone his age.

I retreated two steps and pivoted.

The second strike grazed my sleeve.
The third missed by inches.

Murmurs spread through the observation platforms.

They had expected panic.

Leon's eyes narrowed slightly.

He flicked both wrists, and water mana burst upward from the arena floor around me in six thin pillars, surrounding my position in a ring. The pillars bent inward as they rose, attempting to trap me inside a temporary cage.

A simple containment tactic.

Effective against someone who relied on brute escape.

I let my shoulders tense just enough to look pressured, then slipped through the narrowest opening half a second before the formation sealed.

A few students in the stands made surprised noises.

Leon clicked his tongue.

Leon: "Not bad. For a Green."

That earned a few chuckles.

He gathered more mana.

This time five crystalline lances formed above his shoulders, rotating slowly with controlled pressure. They were denser than his earlier attacks and sharp enough that a direct hit would hurt.

He really did want to embarrass me publicly.

Leon: "Last chance. Yield gracefully."

I looked at him.

Past the arrogance, I could see the familiar shape of insecurity.

He wanted the room's approval.
He wanted Aira's attention.
He wanted to confirm, in front of everyone, that the world's ranking system worked exactly as promised.

People like him were not always cruel because they enjoyed pain.

Sometimes they were cruel because they were terrified of becoming the one beneath someone else.

Haru: "I'd rather continue."

The lances fired.

I raised one hand.

A thin mana shield appeared in front of me-faint, uneven, and deliberately fragile-looking, the kind of defense a Green Circle student might cast in desperation. The first lance shattered against it. The second split past my shoulder. I let the third scrape my upper arm hard enough to tear fabric but not flesh. Then I dropped low, allowing the final two to strike the barrier behind me.

Gasps rose from the stands.

Not because the exchange was spectacular.

Because it wasn't supposed to happen at all.

A Green Circle should not have defended that well against a practiced Blue.

Leon heard the shift in the audience too.

And he hated it.

His smile vanished.

Leon: "Fine."

The temperature in the arena dropped as he pulled more ambient moisture into his mana field. Water gathered around him in spiraling bands, thicker now, denser, more forceful. He was abandoning clean technique for pressure.

Instructor Dren's voice came from outside the barrier.

Instructor Dren: "Varek. Control your output."

Leon heard him. His jaw tightened.

Then he chose pressure anyway.

Three whip-like streams cracked toward me at once from different angles-left, right, above. I retreated across the arena, keeping my footwork efficient but not impossibly so. One stream struck the stone where I had been standing and carved a shallow groove before the arena runes repaired it. Another snapped at my ankles. I jumped. The third clipped my shoulder.

A ripple of pain spread through my arm.

Real enough to be convincing.

I landed near one of the balance markers embedded in the floor, circular glyphs that registered displacement and control during the assessment. Leon noticed immediately and changed strategy. Instead of targeting me directly, he aimed to force me off the marker and claim the first disruption point.

Smart.

He drove a concentrated pulse of water mana into the ground beneath my feet.

I pushed off at the last instant.

The marker flashed as the stone cracked under impact.

**POINT: VAREK**

The panel above the arena lit up.

Cheers came from a few Blue Circle students. One Red near the front clapped twice, bored but approving. The Greens around the lower benches watched the marker instead of Leon, as if the glowing point had confirmed something they had already been taught to expect.

Leon: "There it is."

He thought the match had corrected itself.

I let him believe that.

We circled.

He attacked again, sharper now, less interested in clean form than in momentum. Sheets of water mana folded into blades. Mist spread low across the arena to obscure footing. He was trying to rush me into a mistake before the strange discomfort of the first exchange could take root in the audience.

I parried one strike with a compact reinforcement pulse.
Sidestepped another.
Let a third force me backward.

I could have ended the match in a hundred ways.

A pressure-point disruption to his mana channels.
A muffled collapse of his spell geometry.
A micro-fluctuation in the arena barrier to break his rhythm.
A single tap of force enough to disarm him without anyone understanding what had happened.

Instead, I chose difficulty.

Because looking weak was far harder than being strong.

Leon came in fast, condensing water around his forearm into a hardened striking edge. At close range, elemental shaping like that could be dangerous.

He swung.

I blocked late on purpose, absorbing enough impact to slide backward across the floor.

The audience reacted exactly as expected.

Student: "There!"

Student: "Now he's done."

But Leon had overcommitted.

His weight shifted too far forward.
His centerline opened for a fraction of a second.
His next spell sequence was already forming before he had recovered balance.

Small mistakes.
Common mistakes.
The kind good training was supposed to prevent.

I moved.

Not fast enough to look unnatural.
Just faster than he expected.

I stepped inside his range, redirected his wrist with the minimum possible force, and tapped two fingers against the mana node near his elbow.

A tiny pulse.

Nothing dramatic.

Just enough to interrupt the spell flow in his dominant arm.

His water construct burst apart instantly.

Leon's eyes widened.

I pivoted past him and touched the nearest balance marker with my palm while he was still recovering.

The glyph flashed.

**POINT: AERLEN**

The arena took one collective breath and forgot what to do with it.

A Green Circle had just scored against a Blue.

Not through luck.
Not through a wild outburst.
Through timing.

Leon spun toward me, stunned.

The stands erupted in uneven pieces.

Student: "What?"
Student: "How did he-"
Student: "Did Leon just lose a point?"
Student: "No, he disrupted the channel-"
Student: "Can Greens even do that?"

One of the Greens near the lower bench smiled before he remembered to hide it.

A Blue beside him looked offended on Leon's behalf.

Aira was on her feet before she seemed to realize it.

Her eyes were wide, fixed on me with a look that was no longer simple concern.

It was interest.

Serious interest.

Leon's face flushed.

Not with effort.

With humiliation.

Leon: "Fluke."

I said nothing.

That made it worse.

He attacked with far more force than the assessment required.

Water surged around him in a rising spiral, then crashed forward in a broad wave that raced across the arena floor, forcing me backward. Mist exploded upward. Visibility dropped. Several students in the stands leaned away instinctively as the barrier shimmered under the pressure.

Instructor Dren: "Varek! Reduce output!"

Leon pushed harder.

He was no longer fighting to score.

He was fighting to erase embarrassment.

Through the mist, I tracked him easily by mana flow. His breathing had changed. His spell rhythm was less refined now, more emotional, less precise. He condensed six water blades around himself and launched them in staggered formation-two high, two center, two low.

A pattern designed to overwhelm.

I let my breathing slow.

For the first time since entering the arena, I stopped pretending to be uncertain.

I moved through the attack.

One step left.
Pivot.
Drop.
Turn.

My hand brushed a blade and redirected it just enough to alter its course.
Another passed over my shoulder.
A third struck the floor and dissolved.
The fourth I shattered with a compressed pulse hidden inside an apparently desperate block.
The fifth and sixth missed entirely as I slipped past the centerline of his formation.

The noise in the stands thinned until I could hear water hiss against the barrier.

I registered it only distantly.

Leon saw me emerge from the mist untouched.

And for the first time, fear flickered in his eyes.

Not fear of injury.

Fear of not understanding what he was seeing.

He gathered mana too quickly after that.

Too much, too fast, and with too little discipline.

The spiral around him thickened and destabilized.

I felt the shift instantly.

So did Dren.

Instructor Dren: "Stop casting!"

Leon tried to force the spell into shape anyway.

A compressed sphere of turbulent water mana formed above his right hand, vibrating violently. The structure was incomplete. Unstable. If it burst at that range, he would be the one hurt most.

The arena barrier tensed.

Students in the stands recoiled.

My perception narrowed.

I had choices.

Expose too much and save him perfectly.
Hold back and let him get injured.
Intervene just enough and risk questions.

Mercy was not soft in moments like that.

It was a calculation made with no clean answer.

I stepped forward.

Leon's eyes snapped to mine.

His anger cracked, and fear looked through.

Then I raised my hand and released the smallest fraction of power I could manage without failing.

Not Black Circle power in any true sense.
Not even close.

Just an impossibly precise pulse folded so deeply inside ordinary reinforcement mana that anyone without absurd perception would miss what it really was.

The unstable sphere above Leon's hand folded inward with a wet, airless thump.

No explosion.
No backlash.
No damage.

The water fell as harmless rain.

The arena needed a second to understand it had not just watched someone get hurt.

Leon looked at his hand as if his own magic had become unfamiliar.

Instructor Dren's expression changed.

Only slightly.

But I noticed.

He had seen enough to know that something about that was wrong.

Voices rushed in all at once.

Student: "What just happened?"
Student: "Did Varek cancel his own spell?"
Student: "No, the transfer did something-"
Student: "A first-day Green transfer?"
Student: "That control makes no sense-"

Leon stumbled back a step, looking at his own hand as if it had betrayed him.

Dren entered the arena immediately.

Instructor Dren: "Match over."

The panel above us flashed.

**ASSESSMENT ENDED**

A wave of protest and confusion swept through the stands.

Student: "What? Why?"
Student: "Sir, that's it?"
Student: "He was about to lose!"
Student: "No, Leon would've won-"
Student: "Would he?"

Dren ignored them.

He looked first at Leon.

Instructor Dren: "You lost emotional control, exceeded safe output parameters, and ignored a direct instruction."

Leon opened his mouth. Closed it.

Dren turned to me.

Instructor Dren: "And you demonstrated unusual tactical timing."

Haru: "Thank you, sir."

His eyes stayed on me longer than the comment required.

Then he faced the class.

Instructor Dren: "Observe carefully. Today's lesson is simple. Rank matters. It is not absolute. Any student who uses natural advantage as a substitute for discipline will eventually be embarrassed."

Several Greens looked suddenly a little less small.

Several Blues looked irritated.

Aira was still watching me.

The match should have ended there.

For the class, perhaps it did.

For me, it was the beginning of a problem.

Because attention had weight.
And I could already feel it settling over me.

As the barrier dropped and students began talking over one another, Leon brushed past me hard enough to make the gesture deliberate.

Leon: "You planned that."

Haru: "No. You rushed."

He stopped.

Turned.

For one heartbeat, anger and uncertainty fought visibly in his expression.

Then he walked away.

Aira reached me a moment later.

Aira: "That wasn't normal."

Haru: "For a Green Circle?"

Aira: "For anyone on their first day."

There was no accusation in her voice.

Only amazement.

And something more dangerous than suspicion.

Interest mixed with trust.

I did not know what to do with either.

Haru: "It was timing."

She held my gaze, searching for the edge of the answer I would not give.

Aira: "Maybe. But I think there's more to you than timing, Haru."

Before I could answer, a new presence entered the arena from the upper observation platform.

The arena changed without anyone announcing why.

Conversations broke off mid-word. Students straightened. Even Leon looked up.

At the top of the stairs stood a girl in the formal elite mantle reserved for special-ranking students. Her posture was elegant, her expression composed, and the mana around her felt bright, heavy, and refined enough to press lightly against the skin from a distance.

A **Red Circle** crest glowed at her collarbone.

Not just any Red.

A high-grade one.

She looked down at the arena.
At Dren.
At Leon.
And then at me.

Her gaze lingered.

Not with contempt.

With calculation.

Instructor Dren: "Student Council President."

So this was her.

The one reputation alone had reached even transfer paperwork and hallway whispers.

Aurelis student council did not run dances and decorate notice boards. It controlled student disciplinary referrals, emergency witnesses, club and duel permits, and enough access privileges to make most heirs behave in public.

She descended the steps slowly, each movement controlled.

Rhea: "I apologize for interrupting. I heard there was unusual activity in the assessment hall."

Her voice was calm, polished, and impossible to read.

Instructor Dren: "The matter is handled."

Rhea: "Handled, then. Still, it seems this year's transfer student has already become interesting."

A few students exchanged glances.

I said nothing.

She studied me for one second longer, then smiled very slightly.

It was not a warm smile.

It was the smile of someone who had noticed a thread and intended to keep pulling until the whole pattern revealed itself.

Rhea: "I'll look forward to seeing more."

Then she turned and left with the same controlled pace, leaving half the room pretending not to watch her go.

Aira: "That's not good."

Haru: "Who was that?"

She looked at me as if I had asked the name of a storm everyone else had learned to fear.

Aira: "Rhea Valmont. Student Council President. Red Circle. If she notices you, paperwork starts growing teeth."

I looked back toward the stairs where Rhea had vanished.

My first day at Aurelis was not even over.

Already, hiding had become a group activity.


===== EPISODE 2: The Girl Above the World =====

# Episode 2: **The Girl Above the World**

The rumors started before morning classes did.

It was the problem with elite schools.

Nothing stayed small.

By the time I entered the main courtyard of **Aurelis Arcane Academy**, students were already talking about the previous day's assessment as if it had been a championship duel instead of a classroom exercise.

Some of the versions were merely exaggerated.

Others had already become nonsense.

Student: "He broke Leon's spell with a Green Circle output."

Student: "No, Leon lost control on his own."

Student: "I heard the instructor ended it because the transfer was secretly using a relic."

Student: "Someone said his mana moved strangely."

Student: "Strangely how?"

Student: "No idea. That's what makes it strange."

I walked past them with my bag over one shoulder, expression neutral, pace unhurried.

The safest way to survive attention was to refuse to feed it.

Unfortunately, attention had a way of feeding itself.

A pair of Blue Circle students near the central staircase went quiet as I passed.

One of them looked directly at my concealed crest, then at my face.

Student 1: "That's him."

Student 2: "He still looks ordinary."

Student 1: "That's the unsettling part."

I kept walking.

The silver-leaf trees lining the courtyard shimmered in the early light. Overhead, practice glyphs rotated above the upper towers, marking today's training schedules. Students moved through the paths in clusters of polished uniforms, family insignias, and carefully managed confidence.

And somewhere in all of that, I could already feel the shift.

Yesterday I had been invisible.

Today I was a question.

It was more dangerous.

As I reached the entrance hall, I spotted Aira standing near one of the floating class directories, holding two cups from the academy cafe. She noticed me before I could decide whether to change direction.

She raised one of the cups.

Aira: "I guessed you'd come through this way."

Haru: "You planned for coincidence?"

Aira smiled.

Aira: "I prefer 'efficient friendliness.'"

She stepped closer and offered me the drink.

Aira: "It's tea. I didn't know what you liked, so I picked the least risky option."

I looked at the cup, then at her.

Haru: "Thank you."

Aira: "You're welcome."

There was a small pause.

Aira: "Also, before the day gets any worse, I should warn you that half the academy has decided you're either secretly brilliant or deeply suspicious."

Haru: "Only half?"

That made her laugh.

Aira: "You're getting better at this."

Haru: "At attracting trouble?"

Aira: "At sounding like you're joking."

We started walking together toward the east wing.

That alone was enough to draw glances.

Aira seemed used to them.

I was not sure I ever would be.

Aira took a sip from her own cup and lowered her voice.

Aira: "Leon is furious, by the way."

Haru: "I assumed."

Aira: "He's pretending not to care, which for Leon is somehow louder than normal anger."

Haru: "That seems inconvenient."

Aira: "For you, yes. For me, it's Tuesday."

We entered the corridor just as a new wave of conversation moved through the hall.

Only this time, the tone was different.

It was softer.

Sharper.

More reverent.

I felt the mana before I saw the source.

It was not merely strong.

It was radiant.

A presence like warm sunlight pressing through glass.

The students ahead began moving aside almost instinctively, clearing a path down the center of the corridor. Blue Circles straightened. Red Circles turned with measured interest. Even some instructors paused.

Then she appeared.

She was tall-taller than most of the girls in our year, and tall enough that the elegance of her posture became part of her presence rather than an afterthought. Her hair fell in long waves the color of pale gold touched with amber, catching the morning light as if it belonged to it. Her eyes were clear and luminous, somewhere between honey and sunlight, and when she moved, there was a calm to it that made everyone around her seem hurried by comparison.

Her uniform was the same as everyone else's in design, but on her it looked almost ceremonial. The academy mantle rested neatly over her shoulders, marked with the insignia reserved for the highest-ranked students.

And at her collarbone, unmistakable and brilliant, glowed a **Yellow Circle**.

Not hidden.
Not dimmed.
Not modest.

A true Yellow Circle crest carried itself like a law of nature.

The corridor fell into a respectful hush.

Student 3: "It's her."

Student 4: "Lady Elira."

Student 5: "She's back on campus today?"

Aira noticed the direction of my attention and quietly leaned closer.

Aira: "That's **Elira Vale**."

Haru: "I gathered she was important."

Aira gave me a look.

Aira: "That might be the most restrained sentence anyone has ever said about Elira Vale."

Haru: "Should I know more?"

Aira: "She's a Yellow Circle. One of the youngest high-grade light-affinity magicians in the country. Top-ranked in magical theory, top-ranked in practical control, and somehow still kind enough to make people feel guilty for being awful around her."

Haru: "So everyone admires her."

Aira: "Admire is a gentle word for it."

Elira continued down the corridor, surrounded not by attendants but by open space. No one crowded her. No one dared. And yet unlike the Reds who often wore status like a polished blade, she did not seem to enjoy the way people moved for her.

If anything, she looked faintly uncomfortable with the distance.

Then, to my surprise, her gaze shifted.

And stopped on me.

Only for a second.

But long enough that I felt it.

Not contempt.
Not idle curiosity.

Recognition of anomaly.

Her eyes narrowed very slightly, almost imperceptibly.

Then she passed.

The corridor's breathing seemed to return all at once.

Aira turned toward me with exaggerated seriousness.

Aira: "If you tell me you didn't notice that, I'll be offended on behalf of all observational skill."

Haru: "She looked at me."

Aira: "Yes."

Haru: "People do that here."

Aira stared at me for a moment.

Aira: "You really don't understand how this school works yet."

Haru: "I'm learning."

Aira: "Then lesson one: Yellow Circle students do not casually notice Green Circle transfers."

Haru: "Maybe she was looking past me."

Aira: "Haru."

Haru: "Fine. She was looking at me."

Aira folded her arms.

Aira: "Good. Growth."

We entered homeroom a minute later, and the room's atmosphere sharpened immediately.

Yesterday's curiosity had matured overnight into watchfulness.

Leon Varek was already in his seat, speaking to two other Blue Circle boys in a voice low enough to signal strategy and loud enough to invite overhearing. When he saw me enter beside Aira, the corner of his mouth tightened.

Leon: "Impressive. One lucky assessment and now you travel with company."

Aira: "Good morning to you too, Leon."

Leon: "I'm being civil."

Aira: "Then I'd hate to hear you hostile."

I took my seat without responding.

That seemed to irritate him more.

Instructor Dren arrived soon after and began the lesson on multi-layered reinforcement theory. He said nothing about yesterday's assessment, which was somehow more effective than mentioning it would have been. The class kept glancing at me anyway.

During the lecture, I felt it again.

A strange sensation at the edge of awareness.

Someone watching.

Not from the room.

From above.

When I looked toward the tall windows, there was nothing there but light.

Still, the feeling remained.

---

Lunch at Aurelis was less a meal and more a social sorting ritual disguised as one.

The academy dining hall was vast, divided into elegant tiers and alcoves with panoramic windows overlooking the lower gardens. Students clustered by rank, influence, and confidence. Even table placement carried meaning.

Aira and I had just taken our trays toward a side table that offered relative quiet when the room shifted again.

The same hush as before, though softer now.

People made room.

I did not need to turn to know who it was.

Elira Vale crossed the dining hall with composed steps, sunlight gathering at the edges of her hair where it struck the glass. Up close, the force of her presence was even stranger. Yellow Circle mana was not merely heavy. It was refined to a degree that made lesser currents around it feel unsteady, like candle flames in a bright room.

But there was no arrogance in her expression.

Only attentiveness.

She stopped beside our table.

Half the hall forgot how to breathe.

Her hand hovered near the chair back for half a second. Her gaze moved once across the crowded hall, measuring every eye that had followed her, before returning to us.

Elira: "May I sit here?"

Aira nearly dropped her fork.

Aira: "You're asking us?"

Elira's lips curved faintly.

Elira: "That seemed polite."

Aira looked at me, then back at Elira, then visibly decided she would process this emotional damage later.

Aira: "Yes. Naturally. Please."

Elira sat gracefully across from us.

The room had become painfully attentive while pretending not to be.

Aira, to her credit, recovered first.

Aira: "I'm Aira Solen."

Elira: "I know."

Aira blinked.

Elira turned to me.

Elira: "And you're Haru Aerlen."

Haru: "Yes."

Her golden eyes held mine with a calm steadiness that felt more dangerous than open scrutiny.

Elira: "You asked an interesting question in class yesterday."

Aira stared at me.

Aira: "You asked a question that reached the Yellow Circle lunch network?"

Haru: "Apparently."

Elira's expression softened very slightly.

Elira: "Questions are rarely the problem. The fear of them usually is."

It was not a typical academy answer.

Haru: "Do you believe Black Circle theory has merit?"

A few nearby students almost choked trying to listen discreetly.

Elira lowered her gaze briefly to her tea, then looked back up.

Elira: "I believe the history of magic is incomplete. And I believe most people are comfortable dismissing anything that unsettles the order they benefit from."

Aira went very still.

Aira: "That is not the answer I expected."

Elira: "Most people expect Yellow Circle students to defend the system."

Aira: "Do you?"

Elira smiled, but there was something sad in it.

Elira: "The system defends itself quite well."

Briefly, none of us spoke.

The noise of the dining hall returned at the edges, but distant, as though our table had become its own smaller world.

Elira folded her hands neatly.

Elira: "I also wanted to thank you."

Haru: "For what?"

Elira: "Yesterday. You prevented Leon's spell from collapsing badly enough to injure him."

Aira turned to me so quickly her chair made a sound against the floor.

Aira: "You did what?"

Across the hall, several people were now very openly pretending not to listen.

Haru: "I reacted."

Elira: "With extremely precise timing."

Haru: "So I've been told."

For the first time, Elira's smile became genuine.

Not grand.
Not dazzling.
Just warm.

It changed her whole face.

And for one deeply inconvenient second, I forgot how to answer.

Aira noticed.

She noticed.

Aira looked between us with the expression of someone who had just realized the day might become entertaining in ways she had not planned for.

Elira set down her cup.

Elira: "There is an advanced practical demonstration this afternoon in the upper training gardens. My group has been asked to assist."

Aira: "Your group?"

Elira: "High-tier affinity control."

Aira made a soft noise that translated easily into *that is far above our schedule*.

Elira looked at me again.

Elira: "You should come watch, Haru."

There was no challenge in the invitation.

Only sincerity.

Which somehow made it more dangerous.

Haru: "Why?"

Elira: "Because I think you notice things other people miss."

Aira, now fully committed to her role as witness to my future complications, rested her chin on one hand.

Aira: "He does that."

Haru: "Aira."

Aira: "I'm helping."

Elira's eyes brightened with amusement.

Elira: "Good. Then perhaps both of you can come."

Aira: "Now I definitely have to."

---

The upper training gardens were built on a series of broad terraces overlooking the academy's western cliffs. Ornamental trees lined the paths, and the practice fields were arranged between flowerbeds, crystal pylons, and rune-etched stone circles designed to stabilize high-level mana output.

Students gathered in the observation area well before the demonstration began. Most were upper-year Red and Blue Circles hoping to witness something impressive. A few instructors stood at the edges with the posture of people prepared to intervene if necessary.

Aira and I arrived just as the first display circle activated.

At the center of the terrace, several elite students took their positions. Two high-grade Reds. One specialized Blue. And Elira.

Even standing still, she drew the eye.

The afternoon light fell across her hair and shoulders, turning the gold in her crest almost white at its brightest points. When she lifted one hand, the mana around the terrace changed. It was subtle at first, a soft rise in pressure, then a harmonic resonance through the practice field as the stabilizing runes responded.

Instructor Maren, supervising the demonstration, addressed the crowd.

Instructor Maren: "Today's exercise will focus on synchronized affinity layering under unequal mana conditions. Observe the circle balance, hand transitions, and restraint thresholds."

The Reds began first, weaving complex fire and wind patterns into intersecting rings. The Blue maintained structural water barriers to contain overflow. It was skilled work. Precise. Elegant.

Then Elira stepped into the center node.

Light bloomed.

Not blinding.

Beautiful.

Golden-white threads unfolded from her fingertips and spread across the air like woven silk, threading between the existing spells without disturbing them. She did not overpower the formation. She refined it. Strengthened weak points. Smoothed instability. Elevated everyone else's work simply by touching the structure.

The terrace fell silent.

Aira whispered without taking her eyes off the field.

Aira: "Every time I see her cast, it feels unfair."

Haru: "She's very controlled."

Aira looked sideways at me.

Aira: "That is such a Haru thing to say about something that beautiful."

I kept my gaze on the field.

Haru: "It is beautiful."

Aira blinked.

Aira: "Oh. Good. You do have a functioning heart."

On the field, the demonstration shifted into its second phase. The instructors increased the mana load. One of the Red Circle students lost alignment for a moment, causing the spell structure to tilt. The stabilizing rings vibrated dangerously.

Before anyone else could react, Elira changed the entire pattern.

Her left hand opened. Her right turned.

The golden threads multiplied, crossed, and lifted the failing structure into a new equilibrium so smoothly it looked effortless.

Most of the crowd would remember the spell as flawless.

I remembered the half-breath where it almost wasn't, and the exact calm with which she refused to let someone else's mistake become damage.

The crowd broke into quiet applause.

Elira exhaled and lowered her arms.

Then her gaze moved through the audience.

And found me again.

This time, she smiled openly.

Not to the crowd.

To me.

Aira slowly turned her head.

Aira: "Oh no."

Haru: "What?"

Aira: "Nothing. Just the beginning of trouble."

Haru: "You say that a lot."

Aira: "Because you keep making it true."

After the demonstration ended, students surged forward to congratulate the participants. I would have preferred to leave before becoming part of that movement, but Aira had already been recognized by one of her friends and pulled into a side conversation.

Which left me standing near the terrace railing alone.

For approximately seven seconds.

Then Elira approached.

Up close, with the demonstration over, she somehow felt more human and more dangerous at the same time.

Her expression was calm, but there was a natural softness to it now that the formal distance of the performance was gone.

Elira: "You came."

Haru: "You invited me."

Elira: "Many people accept invitations for the wrong reasons."

Haru: "And what reasons were you expecting from me?"

Elira studied me for a moment, the late afternoon light resting across her hair and shoulders like something that belonged to her naturally.

Elira: "Curiosity, perhaps."

Haru: "Harmless, then."

Elira: "It can be. It can also change everything."

She turned slightly and rested one hand on the stone railing overlooking the western cliffs. Below us, clouds drifted through the lower ravines, and the light from the city far beneath reflected faintly upward through the mana mist.

For a few seconds, neither of us spoke.

The noise from the training terrace continued behind us-students praising the demonstration, instructors giving measured feedback, mana still fading from the practice circles. But around Elira, everything always seemed to narrow into focus.

She had a quiet gravity to her.

Not like authority.

More like a star that did not need to prove it was burning.

Elira: "You weren't impressed in the usual way."

Haru: "There's a usual way?"

Elira's lips curved faintly.

Elira: "Most people watching a Yellow Circle demonstration either admire it openly or resent it privately."

Haru: "And I did neither?"

Elira: "You observed."

It was accurate.

Too accurate.

Haru: "You noticed that?"

Elira turned to face me again.

Elira: "I notice when someone is watching the structure instead of the spectacle."

There was no accusation in her tone.

That somehow made me more cautious, not less.

Haru: "Your control was excellent."

Elira: "That sounds like an academic evaluation."

Haru: "Would you prefer a less academic one?"

This time her smile was immediate.

Warm. Bright. Brief enough to disappear before it became dangerous.

Elira: "I would prefer an honest one."

I looked back toward the training field, where the last traces of golden mana were dissolving into the air.

Haru: "It was beautiful."

When I said it, I meant the spell.

Unfortunately, the silence that followed suggested the sentence had room for interpretation.

Elira's expression softened by something almost too subtle to name.

Elira: "Thank you."

From behind us, I could feel Aira looking in our direction.

I did not turn.

Because if I did, I was certain I would find an expression that would inconvenience me later.

Elira folded her hands lightly in front of her.

Elira: "You really did stop Leon's backlash yesterday."

There it was.

The certainty had finally arrived without pretense.

Haru: "I prevented the situation from getting worse."

Elira: "That isn't a denial."

Haru: "It isn't a confession either."

Briefly, I thought she might press harder.

Instead, she looked out over the cliffside gardens.

Elira: "People assume power is loud."

Haru: "Usually it wants to be noticed."

Elira: "And yours doesn't?"

I went still.

Only inwardly.

Outwardly, I kept my expression calm.

Haru: "My what?"

Elira did not look at me when she answered.

Elira: "Your presence feels inconsistent."

It was a dangerous sentence.

Far more dangerous than suspicion from Leon or curiosity from the class.

Because Elira was not guessing carelessly.

She was perceiving.

Haru: "I'm a Green Circle transfer student. There isn't much room for inconsistency in that."

Elira finally turned back to me.

Elira: "That answer would work better if you believed it."

For the first time since meeting her, I did not have a response ready.

She saw that too.

Her tone gentled.

Elira: "You don't have to tell me anything."

Haru: "Then why say it?"

Elira: "Because sometimes being seen is not the same thing as being exposed."

That caught me off guard more effectively than any attack spell could have.

Most people, if they noticed something unusual, pushed toward it.
Wanted to define it.
Wanted control over it.
Wanted a secret they could use.

But Elira said it as if recognition itself could be an act of care.

I was not sure what to do with that.

Haru: "You're unusually kind for someone people seem to treat like royalty."

She gave a soft, almost amused exhale.

Elira: "That may be the least flattering compliment I've ever received."

Haru: "I meant it well."

Elira: "I know."

She glanced over my shoulder toward the terrace.

Aira was now definitely pretending not to watch us while very clearly watching us.

Elira noticed and smiled.

Elira: "Your friend looks concerned."

Haru: "Aira tends to expect complications."

Elira: "Does she often turn out to be right?"

Haru: "More than I would prefer."

Elira laughed quietly.

The sound was gentle and unguarded, and I understood immediately why people found themselves drawn to her. Not because she was a Yellow Circle. Not because she was beautiful.

Those things were obvious.

It was because despite the aura, the status, the impossible rank, there was something deeply human in the way she listened.

Elira: "May I ask you something?"

Haru: "You already have."

Elira: "Another thing, then."

Haru: "All right."

Her expression became more thoughtful.

Elira: "Why did you ask about Black Circle magicians in class?"

The easier answer would have been curiosity.

The safer answer would have been theory.

For reasons I did not fully understand, I gave her neither.

Haru: "Because I wanted to know how people react to the idea of something beyond their system."

She held my gaze.

Elira: "And what did you learn?"

Haru: "That most people laugh first."

Elira: "And after that?"

Haru: "If the idea threatens the order they trust, they become afraid."

Elira was quiet for a few moments.

Then she nodded once, slowly, as if confirming something to herself rather than agreeing with me.

Elira: "That sounds lonely."

I should not have felt that sentence as strongly as I did.

But I did.

Haru: "Not always."

Elira: "No?"

Haru: "Sometimes there are people who make it less so."

For the first time, Elira looked surprised.

Not by the words themselves.

By the fact that I had said them.

A faint color touched her expression before she looked away toward the garden path.

Elira: "You're quieter than most people here."

Haru: "That usually benefits me."

Elira: "Maybe. But I think silence also makes people underestimate how much you're really saying."

It was possible.

Or maybe she simply heard more than others.

Behind us, the terrace had begun to empty. Instructors were dismissing students. Mana circles were dimming one by one. The air felt cooler now, touched by evening.

Elira stepped back from the railing.

Elira: "There's a moonlit archive hall in the north library wing. Hardly anyone uses it after classes."

Haru: "Should I be concerned about where this invitation is going?"

Her eyes brightened again.

Elira: "Only if you dislike old books, quiet places, and honest conversation."

Haru: "That is suspiciously specific."

Elira: "I thought you might appreciate specific."

She was right.

It was the problem.

Haru: "Are you inviting me to the library?"

For the first time since she had approached the table at lunch, her confidence did not arrive immediately.

Elira: "I do not usually invite people there."

Elira: "I am."

Haru: "People are going to misunderstand that."

Elira: "People misunderstand many things."

Haru: "That doesn't sound reassuring."

Elira: "It wasn't meant to be."

I smiled faintly.

Almost.

Then footsteps approached quickly from the side path.

A familiar, irritated mana signature arrived half a second before the voice.

Leon: "Naturally."

Aira appeared just behind him, looking deeply unimpressed.

Aira: "I told him this was a bad idea."

Leon stopped a few paces away from us, his eyes moving from Elira to me with visible disbelief.

Leon: "Lady Elira, I didn't realize you were entertaining lower-year speculation now."

The temperature of the moment changed immediately.

Elira's expression remained calm, but something in her posture cooled.

Elira: "And I didn't realize my conversations required your approval, Leon."

Aira's mouth twitched, clearly enjoying that more than she intended to admit.

Leon stiffened.

Leon: "That isn't what I meant."

Elira: "Then say what you mean more carefully."

He glanced at me again.

Leon: "With respect, I only think reputations should be guarded. Especially when certain people are enjoying attention they haven't earned."

Aira stepped forward at once.

Aira: "He earned enough to embarrass you in front of an entire assessment hall."

Leon: "Aira."

Aira: "No, go ahead. If you're going to be petty, at least be efficient about it."

Leon's jaw tightened.

I could feel the old humiliation from yesterday rising in him again, sharpened now by the fact that Elira herself was standing here.

Elira, however, did not seem angry.

She seemed disappointed.

That somehow made Leon more uncomfortable than anger would have.

Elira: "If your concern is status, keep it. If your concern is truth, improve your eyesight."

Aira made a small sound that might have been shock or admiration.

Leon went pale, then flushed.

Leon: "You think there's something special about him?"

Elira looked at me for the briefest second before answering.

Elira: "I think dismissing people because their circle is green reflects poorly on everyone who does it."

It was not the same as saying no.

Leon noticed.

His eyes narrowed.

Leon: "Then I'll prove yesterday was luck."

Haru: "You don't need to prove anything to me."

Leon gave a short, humorless laugh.

Leon: "That's generous from someone who's enjoying borrowed mystery."

Aira: "Borrowed mystery is a terrible phrase."

Leon ignored her.

Leon: "There's an interclass combat ladder event in three days. Restricted brackets, official observation, no instructor interference unless necessary. If you're really so composed, enter."

Haru: "I just transferred."

Leon: "Then decline. Publicly, if you want. People can decide for themselves what that means."

Aira: "That is manipulation."

Leon: "That is an invitation."

Elira's voice remained calm.

Elira: "It's coercive and transparent."

Leon turned to her quickly, almost desperate to regain dignity.

Leon: "Then he can refuse without shame, can't he?"

The challenge hung in the evening air.

I didn't want attention.
I didn't want the combat ladder.
I definitely didn't want to give Leon a stage.

But I could already see the shape of the rumor if I declined.

The Green Circle transfer who surprised a Blue once and then hid.

More importantly, I could feel something else.

Eyes.

Watching from farther up the garden path.

Not Leon's allies.
Not idle students.
Something more deliberate.

Before I could isolate the mana signature, it vanished.

Elira noticed the shift in my focus.

Elira: "Haru?"

I returned my attention to the group.

Leon was waiting.
Aira was worried.
Elira was unreadable.

Haru: "Fine."

Aira turned sharply.

Aira: "Fine?"

Leon's expression brightened with vindictive satisfaction.

Leon: "You accept?"

Haru: "I'll enter."

Aira: "It was not the correct answer."

Elira, however, did not look surprised.

She looked thoughtful.

Leon straightened, clearly relieved that events had returned to a shape he understood.

Leon: "Good. Then everyone can stop pretending."

He turned and walked away before anyone could take the moment from him.

Aira watched him go, then looked at me with the expression of someone deciding whether to lecture me, worry about me, or both.

Aira: "You enjoy difficult lives, apparently."

Haru: "Not especially."

Aira: "Then why agree?"

Haru: "Because he would keep pushing otherwise."

Aira: "He will keep pushing anyway."

Haru: "Yes."

Aira closed her eyes for a second.

Aira: "I hate when you're calm like that."

Elira stepped a little closer, her voice lower now.

Elira: "You sensed something just now."

Aira looked between us.

Aira: "What did he sense?"

Haru: "I'm not sure."

It was true.

And not true enough.

Elira's gaze lingered on me.

Elira: "Then be careful."

Haru: "I usually am."

Elira: "No," she said gently. "Usually you're controlled. That isn't always the same thing."

Again, she said things that stayed with me longer than they should have.

Aira looked from Elira to me and sighed in visible surrender.

Aira: "I can already tell my peaceful academic year is gone."

Elira smiled at her.

Elira: "I'll try not to make it worse."

Aira: "That sentence is not comforting when spoken by a Yellow Circle girl standing this close to my extremely suspicious friend."

Haru: "I'm suspicious?"

Aira: "Profoundly."

Elira's laughter was soft and bright.

Briefly, the tension eased.

The sunset washed the terrace in gold, and the wind from the western cliffs carried the fading scent of spell-burnt air and garden flowers.

Then the academy bells rang, low and resonant, calling students back toward the residence halls and evening study blocks.

Elira turned toward the upper path.

Elira: "The library invitation still stands."

Haru: "You're persistent."

Elira: "Only when something matters."

Her eyes met mine for one quiet second.

Then she inclined her head to Aira and began walking away.

Aira waited until Elira had disappeared into the upper garden lights before speaking.

Aira: "Well."

Haru: "Well what?"

Aira folded her arms.

Aira: "A Yellow Circle just invited you to a secret quiet library hall, defended you in public, and looked at you like a riddle she wants to solve gently."

Haru: "That's a very specific interpretation."

Aira: "It's an accurate one."

Haru: "You sound confident."

Aira: "I am. Also, I don't know whether to be worried for you or for the entire academy."

Haru: "That seems dramatic."

Aira: "You literally asked about Black Circle magic on your first day, survived Leon's meltdown, and now have the attention of Elira Vale. Dramatic has already happened."

She started down the terrace path.

Aira: "Come on. If you're going to ruin your life, at least do it after dinner."

I followed.

But as we descended toward the main halls, my thoughts were no longer on Leon.

Or even on the combat ladder.

They were on Elira.

On the way she had looked at me without fear.
On the way she had noticed what others missed.
On the dangerous kindness in her voice when she said being seen was not the same as being exposed.

And beneath all of that, quieter but no less real, was the feeling I had sensed at the edge of the garden.

Someone had been watching.

Not with schoolyard curiosity.

With intent.

The evening lights of Aurelis flickered on one by one below the cliffs.

Far above, hidden behind my false Green Circle crest, the sleeping darkness near my heart gave one slow, silent pulse.

As if reminding me that no matter how gentle this school life tried to become, something older and more dangerous was already moving.


===== EPISODE 3: A Quiet Humiliation =====

# Episode 3: **A Quiet Humiliation**

Three days passed with too many whispers and too little quiet.

The Moonlit Archive invitation remained between Elira and me like a promise postponed, not forgotten. But Leon had forced the next public step, and Aurelis loved nothing more than turning tension into spectacle.

The academy combat ladder arena was louder than usual.

It was Leon's fault.

By the time I arrived at the circular battle hall after classes, half of Aurelis Arcane Academy had already filled the observation tiers. Students leaned over the railings, balancing excitement with the careful posture of people pretending they were only mildly interested. Word had spread quickly.

The Green Circle transfer who had disrupted Leon Varek once by "luck" was stepping into an official bracket match.

To most of them, this was entertainment.

To Leon, it was reputation management.

To me, it was an inconvenience.

Aira and I reached the upper platform together. She looked down at the crowd gathering below, then at me, then back at the crowd as if calculating the scale of the disaster.

Aira: "This is terrible."

Haru: "You sound certain."

Aira: "Because I am. There are too many people here for anything to remain normal."

Haru: "Nothing here has been normal since I arrived."

Aira: "True. But this is a more public kind of not normal."

She looked at me more carefully.

Aira: "You're calm."

Haru: "Should I not be?"

Aira: "That depends. Are you calm because you've made peace with probable humiliation, or calm because you're about to do something that will give me a stress condition?"

Haru: "I don't think I'm that difficult."

Aira gave me a flat look.

Aira: "Haru, I say this with respect. You are the human form of hidden complications."

I smiled faintly.

Almost.

Across the arena floor, Leon was already there.

He stood near the central ring in an immaculate training uniform, sleeves rolled neatly, blond hair catching the overhead light in the exact way he probably hoped it would. Two Blue Circle boys stood with him, nodding as he spoke. From a distance, his posture radiated confidence.

Up close, I could feel the instability beneath it.

He had built too much of himself around the need to be seen as superior. Yesterday's embarrassment had not merely wounded his pride. It had threatened the structure holding his identity together.

That made him more dangerous than usual.

Not stronger.

Just more reckless.

A hush moved through the observation tiers.

I turned.

Elira had arrived.

She descended the eastern stairway with the same effortless composure she seemed to carry into every space. Her academy mantle rested over her shoulders, and the golden light of her Yellow Circle crest glowed softly at her collarbone. Even in a crowded arena full of ambitious young magicians, she drew the eye naturally.

Not because she demanded attention.

Because attention belonged to her the moment she entered.

Aira noticed where I was looking and exhaled slowly.

Aira: "There it is."

Haru: "What?"

Aira: "The exact face people make before their lives become more emotionally inconvenient."

Haru: "I was just looking."

Aira: "Mm."

Elira reached our platform and gave Aira a warm nod before turning to me.

Elira: "You came."

Haru: "I said I would."

Elira: "A rare quality at this school."

Aira: "Keeping promises?"

Elira: "Walking voluntarily into obvious trouble."

Aira: "Then yes, that does sound like Haru."

I ignored both of them with as much dignity as possible.

Elira looked down at the arena and then back at me.

Elira: "You don't seem nervous."

Haru: "I've noticed people keep expecting that."

Elira's expression softened into a quiet smile.

Elira: "People usually fear public judgment more than combat."

Haru: "Do you?"

For a brief moment, her eyes changed.

Not dramatically.

Just enough to reveal the truth beneath her grace.

Elira: "I used to."

That answer landed more deeply than I expected.

Before I could respond, a clear amplified voice rang through the arena.

Announcer: "Combat Ladder Preliminary Match: Leon Varek of Year Two, Section Three versus Haru Aerlen, transfer student of Year Two, Section Three. Standard academy restrictions apply. Non-lethal engagement only. Barrier interference forbidden. Excessive force subject to disciplinary review."

Aira folded her arms tightly.

Aira: "If you get injured, I'll be angry."

Haru: "That seems backwards."

Aira: "It's efficient."

Elira's gaze remained on me.

Elira: "Be careful."

Her voice was quiet.

Simple.

And somehow more difficult to ignore than any warning I had received since arriving.

Haru: "I will."

Then I stepped down toward the arena.

---

The barrier rose around us in a ring of pale blue light.

The combat ladder arena was larger than the assessment hall, built for official interclass matches and watched by multiple instructors stationed at the perimeter. The floor was divided into concentric circles etched with response glyphs that measured pressure, displacement, and mana density.

Leon stood opposite me with a practiced smile.

But it was thinner than before.

Less natural.

Leon: "I'm glad you accepted."

Haru: "You sounded like you'd be difficult if I didn't."

That earned a few laughs from the stands.

Leon's smile tightened.

Leon: "Try to keep your confidence after this starts."

Haru: "I'll do my best."

The match official raised one hand.

Official: "Begin."

Leon attacked instantly.

No testing strike this time.

No probing motion.

He opened with force.

Three compressed streams of water mana surged toward me in a triangular pattern, one aimed high, one center, one sweeping low across the floor to attack my footing. The pressure was sharper than during the assessment. He had come prepared to overwhelm quickly.

I stepped once to the side.

The first stream missed.

I tilted my shoulder to let the second skim past.

Then I lifted one hand and cut the third apart with a concentrated mana edge fine enough that most people in the audience would mistake it for reinforcement dispersion.

The sweep dissolved into harmless spray.

The arena murmured.

Leon's eyes narrowed.

He followed immediately with a broader construct-curving walls of water rising from both sides to box me inward while a line of sharpened droplets formed overhead.

A trapping sequence.

Decent.

Predictable.

I had intended to keep the match controlled, modest, just enough to win clearly.

Then I saw Leon's expression.

Not focused.

Not disciplined.

Hungry.

He did not want victory.

He wanted to erase me.

He wanted the Green Circle transfer humiliated so thoroughly that no one would ever speak of the assessment again.

And perhaps, after the past few days of mockery and pressure, some colder part of me decided I was no longer interested in protecting his ego.

Not entirely.

But enough.

I let a little more power rise.

Not much.

Still nowhere near enough to expose the truth.

But more than I had used before.

Enough that the false limits of my Green Circle mask stretched uncomfortably around the output.

Enough that the air around my hands felt sharper.

Enough that the sleeping darkness beneath my chest pulsed once, pleased at being noticed.

Leon drove the water walls inward.

I moved forward.

Straight through the narrowing gap.

My hand touched the left wall of water mana.

For the audience, it looked like I broke through by timing and reinforcement.

For anyone with exceptional perception, it would have felt stranger-like the spell had simply ceased to be allowed to exist where my mana touched it.

The wall collapsed.

Leon's eyes widened.

I crossed the remaining distance between us before his next sequence fully formed.

His reflexes were good. He tried to pivot, drawing a hardened water blade along his forearm for close-range defense.

I caught his wrist.

The entire arena went silent.

Not because catching a wrist was impressive.

Because I did it too cleanly.

Too easily.

Leon pushed mana into the construct, trying to force the blade forward.

I tightened my grip by a fraction.

The water blade shattered.

Leon: "What-"

I stepped inside his guard, touched two fingers lightly to the center of his chest, and released a compressed pulse of force.

Not enough to injure.

More than enough to send him backward.

Leon flew across the arena, hit the floor hard, and skidded into the outer ring with all the dignity of a dramatic lesson.

Gasps exploded through the stands.

Leon coughed, shoved himself up on one elbow, and stared at me in disbelief.

The silence lasted only a second before the arena erupted.

Student 1: "No way."

Student 2: "He sent Leon flying."

Student 3: "That wasn't Green Circle output."

Student 4: "Then what was it?"

Student 5: "Did he strengthen his body first?"

Student 6: "I didn't even see the transition."

On the platform above, Aira was standing.

Her eyes were wide, somewhere between shock and vindication.

Beside her, Elira had gone very still.

Not alarmed.

Focused.

Leon got to his feet, furious now, dignity burned away.

Water mana surged around him in spiraling bands.

Leon: "You think one hit means anything?"

Haru: "No."

I looked at him calmly.

Haru: "But I think the next one will."

The arena reacted to that immediately.

A few students laughed in disbelief.
